6S Success · Home Edition · Field Manual v2.0 · 2026-07-20

The 6S Home Reset:
Room-by-Room Micro Zone SOP

A complete operating manual for transforming a home one micro zone at a time. Every zone is engineered to finish in 15–90 minutes, follows the same six-step discipline, and ends in a visible, auditable change of state — so progress is measured in finished zones, never in exhausted afternoons.

20
Rooms
114
Micro zones
684
6S activities
98h 00m
Total program
Method

The 6S cycle, defined

Fig. 1 — The 6S cycle. Six steps, always in order, applied to one micro zone at a time. The sequence compounds: each step halves the work of the next.

Step 1 · Seiri

Sort

Separate the necessary from the unnecessary.

Sorting is a decision discipline, not a tidying pass. Every item in the zone faces one question — does it serve this zone's primary function? — and gets one of five verdicts: keep, relocate, donate, recycle, or discard. The professional standard is a decision in under ten seconds per item; anything slower goes into a dated holding bin so momentum never stalls. When Sort is done well, the remaining steps take half the time.

Step 2 · Seiton

Straighten

A home for everything, chosen by how it's used.

Straightening is ergonomic design at household scale. Items earn their location by frequency (daily at hand height, weekly at knee-to-shoulder, seasonal high or remote), by sequence (things used together live together), and by physics (heavy low, sharp guarded, fragile protected). The test of a good home: you can retrieve and return the item in one motion, without moving anything else.

Step 3 · Seiso

Shine

Clean to inspect, not just to impress.

In lean practice, cleaning is inspection in disguise. Work top-down and back-to-front so debris falls onto surfaces you haven't cleaned yet, match the chemistry to the surface, and let dwell time do the work a sponge can't. While your hands clean, your eyes audit: the water stain that signals a slow leak, the frayed cord, the hinge starting to sag. Shine finds small problems while they're still small.

Step 4 · Anzen

Safety

The step that protects everything else.

Safety converts the zone from 'looks fine' to 'verified safe.' The sweep covers eight hazard families: egress and access, stability and tip-over, chemicals and storage compatibility, heat, electricity, sharps, moisture and slip, and lifting strain. The rule is correct-or-control: fix hazards on the spot where possible; where not, contain the risk and put the repair on a dated list. A blocked exit or an overloaded outlet outranks every cosmetic improvement in the room.

Step 5 · Seiketsu

Standardize

Make the right state obvious at a glance.

Standardizing is how a one-time cleanup becomes a permanent system. Labels name the homes, quantity limits cap the clutter ('no more than three mugs on the shelf'), and a reference photo defines done better than any paragraph. The measure of a good standard: a family member — or a future you on a tired Tuesday — can restore the zone perfectly without asking a single question.

Step 6 · Shitsuke

Sustain

Design the habit, don't rely on willpower.

Sustain is where most home systems die, because it's treated as a virtue instead of a design problem. The fix is mechanical: name one owner per zone, anchor the reset to a trigger that already happens (after dinner, before the school run, Sunday coffee), and run a two-minute audit on a set cadence. If a zone keeps failing its audit, the standard is wrong — redesign the zone, don't blame the household.

Decision discipline

The Sort decision, mapped

Sorting speed is the single best predictor of whether a reset finishes. This tree removes deliberation: one item in hand, one question, five verdicts, ten seconds.

Fig. 2 — Sort decision tree. Print it, tape it inside the first cabinet you reset, and let it referee every keep-or-toss debate in the house.

Operating principles

Rules of the reset

Work the zone, not the room

A room overwhelms; a micro zone finishes. Every zone in this manual is sized to 15–90 minutes and ends in a visible, auditable change of state. Finish one completely before touching the next — a finished zone teaches the household what the standard looks like.

Follow the S-sequence in order

The six steps compound. Sorting before straightening means you only design homes for what stays. Shining before standardizing means the reference photo captures the true target state. Skipping ahead is the most common cause of rework.

Touch it once

Each item gets picked up one time and moved to its verdict — kept home, relocation box, donation bag, recycling, or trash. Piles labeled 'deal with later' are inventory without an owner, and inventory without an owner becomes clutter again by Friday.

The floor is not storage

In lean facilities, nothing lives on the floor without a marked footprint. At home the same rule prevents trip hazards, makes cleaning fast, and keeps circulation paths honest. If it must live on the floor, give it a defined footprint — a mat, a bin, a taped-out corner of the garage.

Standards before containers

Buying bins is the most seductive way to skip the hard thinking. Sort first, set quantity limits first — then measure what remains and buy (or repurpose) exactly the containers the standard requires. Most zones need fewer containers after 6S, not more.

Make problems visible

Open shelving where accuracy matters, clear bins for backstock, labels facing out, one-in-one-out rules at capacity limits. A well-standardized zone announces its own drift — you can see 'wrong' from the doorway, which is what makes the two-minute audit possible.

Planning

Where the effort goes

Plan the program like a project: start with the entryway (small, fast, daily payoff), take the kitchen and baths early for momentum, and schedule the garage and workshop as dedicated sessions — they are the deep water of the program.

Fig. 3 — Full-reset time by room, computed from the 684 activity estimates in this manual. Times assume one focused adult; a second pair of hands roughly halves elapsed time on rooms over three hours.

Reading the SOPs

How to use this manual

Each room section contains: an expert brief on how the room fails and how it wins; three discipline-tagged pro tips (Organizer, Cleaning, Lean); and one SOP block per micro zone. Each zone block gives its function, completed state, an expert cue, a color-coded time spine showing how the minutes split across the six steps, and the full activity table — task, inputs, output state, and time. Work top to bottom, one zone per session, and photograph the finished state: that photo is your standard.

Room 01 of 20

Entryway

5 zones · 3h 45m full reset

The entryway is the household's air lock — everything the outside world sends in passes through it twice a day. Its zones fail not from dirt but from unmade decisions: mail without a verdict, shoes without a cap, keys without a home. Run this room first; it is small, fast, and the daily payoff builds belief in the whole system.

Organizer tipCap every category with a number, not a feeling: one coat per person on hooks, two pairs of shoes per person at the door, everything else upstairs or in the closet.
Cleaning tipThe entry mat does 80% of the room's cleaning. Vacuum it every reset, hose and dry it monthly, and replace it the moment it stops trapping grit — a worn mat exports dirt to every floor in the house.
Lean tipTreat mail as a single-piece flow: it goes from hand to verdict (act, file, recycle) in one touch. A mail pile is a queue, and queues in the entryway grow without limit.

Zone 1 of 5 · 45 min
Primary function — Receive and launch daily essentials
Completed state — A clear arrival surface with only current-day essentials in defined homes and no loose paper
Common items & storage — keys, wallet, sunglasses, phone, mail, handbag, backpack, work badge. Typical storage approach: small tray, mail sorter, labeled hooks, charging dock.
Expert cuePlace the tray where your hand naturally lands on entry — moving a key bowl 30 cm from the natural drop point is the difference between a system and a suggestion.

Room 02 of 20

Kitchen

7 zones · 7h 45m full reset

The kitchen is the highest-throughput workcell in the home, and it rewards 6S more than any other room. Every minute of prep, cooking, and cleanup crosses these seven zones, so small ergonomic wins compound daily. Sequence matters here: do the sink zone early — it is the recovery point every other zone drains into.

Organizer tipArrange by workflow triangle: prep tools within one step of the prep counter, cooking utensils within arm's reach of the stove, and nothing in the triangle that isn't used weekly.
Cleaning tipDegrease before you sanitize — sanitizer can't reach through grease film. Warm water and dish soap first, then disinfectant with full label dwell time on food-contact surfaces.
Lean tipThe counter is production space, not storage. Every appliance that lives on it must earn its footprint with at least three uses a week; everything else goes to a cabinet garage or the pantry.

Primary Prep Counter

Zone 1 of 7 · 60 min
Primary function — Prepare food on an open work surface
Completed state — A mostly open counter with only active daily-prep tools and at least one full clear work area
Common items & storage — cutting boards, knives, mixing bowls, small appliances, oils, utensils. Typical storage approach: counter boundary, knife storage, appliance homes.
Expert cueClear counter is production capacity: a 60 cm clear run of workspace is the minimum viable prep zone — protect it like a fire lane.

Cooking Zone

Zone 2 of 7 · 75 min
Primary function — Cook at the range, oven, or cooktop
Completed state — Frequently used cooking tools are within safe reach and the cooktop area is clear

Room 03 of 20

Pantry

5 zones · 4h 15m full reset

A pantry is inventory management wearing a wooden costume. Its failure modes are the classic ones — overstock, expired stock, and stock nobody can see — and its fixes are classic too: first-in-first-out rotation, visible quantities, and a hard boundary between working shelf and backstock. A well-run pantry cuts grocery spend because you stop buying what you already own.

Organizer tipDecant only where it pays: flour, sugar, rice, cereal. Everything else keeps its label — the package is free information about expiry dates and cooking instructions.
Cleaning tipCrumbs are livestock feed. Vacuum shelf corners and wipe with mild soap every reset, and inspect flour and grain containers for pantry moths while you shine.
Lean tipRun FIFO on every shelf: new stock loads behind, old stock pulls forward. Date-mark backstock with a marker on the lid — if you can't read the date from the doorway, it isn't marked.

Room 04 of 20

Dining Room

5 zones · 3h 45m full reset

The dining room's central risk is mission drift: the table becomes a desk, the buffet becomes a filing cabinet, and dinner moves to the couch. This SOP restores the room to its actual function — shared meals — and gives the flat surfaces the quantity limits they need to stay clear between them.

Organizer tipThe table's standing inventory is a centerpiece and nothing else. Homework, mail, and projects get a rolling caddy that arrives and leaves with the activity.
Cleaning tipDust the light fixture and chair rungs before the table — top-down order — and condition wood surfaces quarterly so spills bead instead of soaking.
Lean tipStore dishware by pull frequency, not by set: the plates you use daily belong at hand height in the kitchen; the china cabinet holds occasion-ware only, displayed and dust-controlled.

Room 05 of 20

Living Room

6 zones · 4h 00m full reset

The living room is shared territory with no single owner, which is exactly why it drifts. The remedy is visible standards everyone can read: a basket per category, a cable path per device, a clear circulation route wide enough for the vacuum. Aim for a room that resets in five minutes, because it will need to, nightly.

Organizer tipOne basket per media category — remotes, throws, current magazines — and the basket's rim is the quantity limit. When it doesn't close or stack, something leaves.
Cleaning tipVacuum upholstery seams and under cushions monthly; that's where crumbs, coins, and allergens concentrate. Rotate and flip cushions on the same cadence to even the wear.
Lean tipRoute and label cables once, with velcro ties and a labeled power strip, and the media center stays auditable — you can spot a foreign cable or a missing remote at a glance.

Room 06 of 20

Family Room

6 zones · 5h 15m full reset

The family room hosts the most competing activities per square meter in the house — media, play, games, crafts, charging — so its 6S depends on hard zone boundaries. Every activity gets a home base and a closing routine; the room works when a five-year-old can put it away, and only then.

Organizer tipToy rotation beats toy storage: a third of the toys live here, two-thirds rest in labeled bins elsewhere. Fewer visible choices means deeper play and a three-minute pickup.
Cleaning tipDisinfect high-touch play surfaces and controllers weekly — they carry more biological load than the bathroom doorknob — and wash blanket-basket contents monthly.
Lean tipGive every game and puzzle a completeness check on reset day: missing-piece games are defects in inventory. Repair, cannibalize, or discard — never reshelve a broken set.

Room 07 of 20

Primary Bedroom

6 zones · 4h 45m full reset

Sleep science and lean practice agree on the primary bedroom: fewer visible items, lower cognitive load, better rest. The zones here run from the two-minute nightstand to the ninety-minute closet, and the closet is the boss fight — schedule it when you're fresh, and let the seasonal-rotation standard do the heavy lifting afterward.

Organizer tipThe nightstand standard is five items or fewer on top. Everything that supports sleep stays; everything that competes with it — especially the charging phone — finds a home across the room.
Cleaning tipWash pillows (not just cases) quarterly and vacuum the mattress surface and under-bed with the upholstery tool — dust mites concentrate where you breathe all night.
Lean tipRun the reversed-hanger experiment for one season: turn all hangers backward, flip them as items are worn, and let the unflipped hangers make your donation decisions for you.

Room 08 of 20

Guest Bedroom

5 zones · 3h 05m full reset

A guest room has two states — museum and hotel — and 6S keeps it permanently ready to flip. The standard is hotel logic: neutral surfaces, empty drawer space, and a small welcome kit, so that hosting requires fifteen minutes of freshening rather than an afternoon of excavation.

Organizer tipReserve genuine guest capacity: one empty drawer, six empty hangers, and clear nightstand space. A guest room that stores your overflow is a storage room with a bed in it.
Cleaning tipEven unused rooms accumulate dust film and stale air. Between guests, run a monthly ten-minute shine — surfaces, sill, and a linen refresh — so the room never develops that unused smell.
Lean tipStage a standardized welcome kit (water glass, tissues, charger, wifi card, spare blanket) in one labeled bin. Setup becomes a checklist, not a scavenger hunt.

Room 09 of 20

Kids Bedroom

6 zones · 5h 30m full reset

A child's bedroom is a training ground for every 6S habit they'll ever have, which means the standards must be designed for their height, their reading level, and their attention span. Picture labels beat word labels, open bins beat lidded ones, and a closing routine beats every lecture ever delivered.

Organizer tipSet homes at kid height: daily clothes in the bottom drawers, favorite toys on the lowest shelves. If a child needs an adult to put something away, the system — not the child — has failed.
Cleaning tipMake shine a two-song job: one song for surfaces with a microfiber cloth, one for floor pickup. Kids sustain what they can finish inside their attention span.
Lean tipPhotograph the standard state and tape the photo inside the door at eye level. 'Match the picture' is an audit a six-year-old can run without any adult arbitration.

Room 10 of 20

Nursery

6 zones · 4h 05m full reset

The nursery is the one room where 6S is genuinely about safety first and aesthetics a distant second. Every standard here serves a tired adult operating one-handed in the dark: supplies within reach of the changing pad, a bare-crib sleep standard, and chemical and cord hazards engineered out rather than managed.

Organizer tipDesign the changing station for one-handed reach: diapers, wipes, and cream inside a 45-centimeter arc of the pad, restocked to a par level every evening.
Cleaning tipUse fragrance-free, infant-safe cleaners only, and launder crib linens on hot. Wipe the changing pad after every use; deep-clean it on the weekly reset.
Lean tipRun diaper backstock on a two-bin system: when the open bin empties, the reserve bin moves forward and the shopping list gets the trigger. You will never run out at 2 a.m. again.

Room 11 of 20

Primary Bathroom

7 zones · 6h 00m full reset

Bathrooms concentrate the home's moisture, chemistry, and expiry-date problems into its smallest rooms. The primary bath rewards ruthless sorting — cosmetics and medicines expire faster than anyone believes — and a shine discipline built on dwell time, ventilation, and drying the wet zones daily.

Organizer tipCounter standard: daily-use items only, corralled on one tray you can lift with one hand to clean under. Everything used less than daily lives in a drawer or cabinet.
Cleaning tipSpray tub and shower cleaner first, then clean the rest of the room while it dwells — chemistry needs minutes, not muscle. Finish by squeegeeing glass and running the fan 20 minutes.
Lean tipCheck expiry dates on every Sort pass: most cosmetics carry a 6–24 month open-jar symbol, and expired sunscreen and medicine are defects, not inventory. Dispose of medicines via take-back, never the toilet.

Room 12 of 20

Guest Bathroom

5 zones · 3h 00m full reset

The guest bath is the room visitors judge and the household forgets. Its 6S is mercifully small — five zones, hotel-grade standards — and its sustain trigger is simple: a five-minute reset before anyone visits and a weekly shine whether anyone does or not.

Organizer tipStock it like a hotel: backup toilet paper visible without hunting, fresh hand towel, soap above half-full, and a small bin — and nothing personal on the counter.
Cleaning tipEven low-traffic toilets grow biofilm rings. Weekly bowl treatment with full dwell time, plus a monthly pour of water down seldom-used drains to keep the trap sealed against sewer gas.
Lean tipLaminate a five-line reset checklist inside the vanity door. Guest-ready becomes a two-minute verified state, not a scramble triggered by the doorbell.

Room 13 of 20

Laundry Room

6 zones · 4h 00m full reset

The laundry room is a genuine production line — sort, wash, dry, fold, return — and it jams exactly where factories jam: at the fold step, where work-in-progress piles up. This SOP fixes the flow with staged sorting, a clear folding surface with a same-day rule, and machines that get maintained like the equipment they are.

Organizer tipSort at the source with a three-bag hamper station (lights, darks, delicates) so a load starts machine-ready. The floor in front of the washer is a landing zone, not a queue.
Cleaning tipMachines need shine too: empty the lint trap every load, scrub the detergent drawer and door gasket monthly, and run a hot cleaning cycle to stop mildew odor at its source.
Lean tipInstitute the same-day fold rule — a dryer buzzer is an andon signal, not a suggestion. Folded-and-returned inside 24 hours is the standard that keeps WIP at zero.

Room 14 of 20

Home Office

6 zones · 6h 00m full reset

The home office is where physical clutter and digital debt shake hands. Its 6S extends the method to paper flow — act, file, or shred, with one inbox tray as the only legal landing spot — and to the desk itself, which is a workstation with a standard, not a horizontal shelf with a keyboard.

Organizer tipDesk-surface standard: the tools of the current task plus one inbox tray. When the task changes, the desk resets — the standard is a photograph, not a mood.
Cleaning tipElectronics shine safely: powered down, microfiber cloth, screen-safe cleaner sprayed on the cloth never the screen, and compressed air for the keyboard monthly.
Lean tipGive paper a one-touch triage — act, file, shred — and empty the inbox tray on a Friday cadence. A filing backlog is hidden work; the tray's rim is its WIP limit.

Room 15 of 20

Garage

7 zones · 10h 15m full reset

The garage is the home's warehouse, and it fails like one: no aisles, no addresses, no rotation. The transformation lever is vertical — walls and overhead take the storage, the floor keeps only wheeled and parked things — plus honest sorting of the museum of abandoned projects. Budget more time here than anywhere else, and take before photos; the payoff deserves evidence.

Organizer tipZone the walls by activity — auto, garden, sport, tools — and give every hook and shelf an address. The parking footprint is sacred: if the car can't enter, the garage has failed its primary function.
Cleaning tipDegrease the floor once properly (degreaser, stiff brush, absorbent on stains), then sweep monthly. A clean floor makes new leaks — car, water heater, chemicals — instantly visible.
Lean tipOverhead racks hold only labeled, dated, seasonal bins under 15 kg. If a bin comes down less than once a year two years running, its contents are donation candidates by rule.

Primary Workbench

Zone 1 of 7 · 90 min
Primary function — Support repairs, assembly, and household projects
Completed state — The work surface is mostly clear and only one active project occupies the zone
Common items & storage — hand tools, project parts, adhesives, fasteners, bench tools. Typical storage approach: project tray, tool shadowing, bench boundary.
Expert cueEnd every session with a cleared bench — the bench is capacity, and tomorrow's project deserves a clean start with visible tool status.

Room 16 of 20

Workshop

6 zones · 7h 45m full reset

The workshop is where 6S was born — Toyota's factories, scaled to a bench. Shadow boards, point-of-use tooling, and hard chemical segregation aren't aesthetics here; they're the difference between a hobby that builds things and one that loses fingers. The Safety pass in this room is the most important fifteen minutes in the manual.

Organizer tipShadow-board the hand tools: outline every silhouette so a missing tool announces itself before cleanup ends. Point-of-use beats category — the square lives at the saw, not in a drawer of squares.
Cleaning tipSawdust is a slip hazard, a fire load, and a finish-ruiner. Vacuum rather than sweep (sweeping airborne-launches the fines), and empty dust collection at every session's end.
Lean tipStore finishes and solvents in a fire-rated cabinet, rags in a sealed metal can — oily rags self-ignite — and stage PPE at the door so wearing it costs zero extra steps.

Room 17 of 20

Mudroom

6 zones · 4h 15m full reset

The mudroom is the entryway's industrial-strength sibling: same launch-and-land function, triple the throughput, plus pets, sports gear, and weather. Its standards must survive a wet Tuesday at 7:40 a.m. — which means hooks over hangers, open bins over lidded ones, and a floor that forgives.

Organizer tipOne labeled hook-and-bin column per family member turns 'whose is this?' into a solved problem, and makes the owner of any drift instantly visible.
Cleaning tipTrap grit at the threshold: bristle mat outside, absorbent mat inside, boot tray for wet footwear. Vacuum the trays and shake the mats every reset — grit is sandpaper for your floors.
Lean tipStage tomorrow tonight: bags packed on hooks, shoes in the launch position, signed forms in the bag. The morning rush is a process problem, and it is solvable.

Room 18 of 20

Hall Closet

5 zones · 4h 15m full reset

The hall closet is the household's dark warehouse — highest density, lowest visibility, no owner. Its 6S rests on sightlines and stock control: clear bins, labels facing out, par levels for consumables, and a strict eviction rule for anything that merely 'ended up' there.

Organizer tipFold linens to one uniform footprint and store sets nested inside their own pillowcase — one grab equals one complete bed change, and the shelf stays legible.
Cleaning tipClosed dark spaces breed mustiness: empty and wipe shelves on the reset, and leave the door cracked after cleaning sprees so trapped humidity escapes.
Lean tipSet par levels for paper goods and bulbs — min and max quantities written on the shelf edge. Below min triggers the shopping list; above max means stop buying it.

Room 19 of 20

Stair Landing

3 zones · 1h 20m full reset

The stair landing is a circulation space that clutter loves precisely because no one owns it. Its 6S is short and strict: the path stays clear at full width, the 'going upstairs anyway' pile gets a single contained basket, and the safety pass — lighting, handrail, tread edges — protects the most injury-prone route in the house.

Organizer tipIf items must stage here, allow exactly one stair basket per direction with a same-day empty rule. The steps themselves are never storage — not for one book, not for one morning.
Cleaning tipVacuum tread edges and corners where grit collects, and wipe the handrail — it's a high-touch surface that almost never sees a cloth.
Lean tipAudit the landing at light-switch height: bulbs at full brightness, switch reachable at both ends, nothing casting a shadow on the top or bottom step.

Room 20 of 20

Patio or Deck

6 zones · 5h 00m full reset

Outdoor zones live under a maintenance clock that indoor rooms don't: UV, moisture, and temperature swings turn deferred care into replacement costs. This room's 6S leans hard on Shine-as-inspection — checking rail integrity, board condition, and grill grease while cleaning — and on a seasonal Standardize that matches storage to the calendar.

Organizer tipStore cushions and textiles in a ventilated deck box the moment they're dry — UV and dew age fabric faster than use ever will. Covers go on furniture that stays out.
Cleaning tipDegrease the grill's grease tray and drip pan every few cooks, not every few months — accumulated grease is the number-one cause of grill fires.
Lean tipRun the seasonal changeover as a checklist with dated photos: what's out, what's covered, what's stored. Spring setup becomes an hour of restoration instead of a weekend of archaeology.

Keeping it won

The Sustain rhythm

A reset is an event; a system is a rhythm. This is the cadence that keeps 114 zones standing without heroics — none of it depends on motivation, all of it rides on triggers and two-minute audits.

Fig. 4 — The sustain cadence. Daily closes, weekly audits, monthly room reviews, seasonal changeovers.

Appendix A

The two-minute zone audit

Stand at the zone. Compare against the reference photo. Check the line that applies — every unchecked line is either fixed now (under two minutes) or written down with a date.

  • ☐ Every surface matches its reference photo, or the drift and its cause are noted.
  • ☐ Every item visible has a labeled home; nothing is resting 'for now.'
  • ☐ Quantity limits hold: baskets close, trays fit, caps respected.
  • ☐ Floors and circulation paths are clear at full width; nothing on stairs.
  • ☐ No expired stock: food, medicine, cosmetics, and chemicals checked on rotation.
  • ☐ Chemicals stored in original containers, segregated, secured from children.
  • ☐ Egress clear, stacks stable, cords sound, heavy items low, wet zones dry.
  • ☐ Backstock is dated and rotating FIFO; par levels visible where set.
  • ☐ Each zone has a named owner and a reset trigger that actually fires.
  • ☐ Zones failing two consecutive audits are flagged for standard redesign — not blame.
Appendix B

Glossary

Micro zone
A bounded slice of a room — a drawer, a counter, a cabinet — sized so a full 6S pass completes in 15–90 minutes and ends in an auditable change of state.
Primary function
The single job a zone exists to do. Every keep/relocate decision in Sort is tested against it.
Holding bin
A dated container for genuine maybes during Sort. Reviewed at 30 days; anything untouched is donated by rule, not by re-deliberation.
Home
The one defined location where an item lives, chosen by frequency, sequence, and safe access, and marked so return is unambiguous.
Par level
The set minimum and maximum quantity for a consumable. Below min triggers restock; above max stops purchasing.
FIFO
First in, first out. New stock loads behind, old stock pulls forward — the rotation rule that keeps food, medicine, and supplies from expiring in the dark.
Two-bin system
Backstock control with an open bin and a reserve. The reserve moving forward is the automatic restock signal.
Shadow board
A storage surface with each tool's silhouette marked, making any missing item visible at a glance.
Quantity limit
A visible cap on how much of a category a home may hold — the rim of the basket, the edge of the tray, a written number.
Reset trigger
An existing daily event (after dinner, before the school run) that a zone's reset is anchored to, so sustain runs on routine instead of willpower.
Two-minute audit
A timed pass comparing a zone to its reference photo and standard. Pass, or note the drift and its cause — repeated failure means the standard needs redesign.
WIP limit
A cap on work-in-progress — one project out on the craft table, one basket per stair. Queues without limits grow without limits.
